It feels like my brain will just latch onto whatever is in front of me. Like, if I'm driving, I start worrying about getting into an accident or if the car is making a weird noise. Or if my wife is running late coming home from the store, I'm immediately convinced something terrible happened to her. It's just... always something. The retirement is definitely loud right now, but the worrying itself just seems to cover everything.\n\nDoctor: When you’re in these periods of worry, do you often feel restless or on edge?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say that. I don't really feel restless or... jumpy, if that's what you mean. I'm not pacing the floors or anything like that. It's really just the thoughts that won't stop circling, but physically, I haven't really noticed feeling on edge.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel easily fatigued?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. I try to reason with myself, you know, the way I would with a nervous patient in the chair. But it doesn't seem to work on me. One concern just leads right into the next one, and... eventually I just end up checking my emails, reviewing old notes, or asking the clinic the same questions all over again instead of just being able to let it go.\n\nDoctor: Do your worries mostly center on your retirement and the changes around it?\n\nPatient: Well, the retirement is a big part of it right now, but honestly, it's not just that. It feels like my brain will just latch onto whatever is in front of me. Like, if I'm driving, I start worrying about getting into an accident or if the car is making a weird noise. Or if my wife is running late coming home from the store, I'm immediately convinced something terrible happened to her. It's just... always something. The retirement is definitely loud right now, but the worrying itself just seems to cover everything.\n\nDoctor: When you’re in these periods of worry, do you often feel restless or on edge?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say that. I don't really feel restless or... jumpy, if that's what you mean. I'm not pacing the floors or anything like that. It's really just the thoughts that won't stop circling, but physically, I haven't really noticed feeling on edge.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel easily fatigued?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. By midafternoon, I just feel completely worn out. And it's a kind of tired that a nap or taking it easy in the morning doesn't really fix. It's gotten to the point where even simple things I usually enjoy, like weeding the garden or standing at my easel to paint, just feel... heavier than they used to. I just don't have the energy for them anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed lately?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say I feel depressed. I mean, I'm tired, and the worrying is exhausting, but I don't feel sad or down in the dumps. I still want to do things, like my painting or working in the yard, I just... don't have the energy for it right now because my mind is always going. But no, I don't feel depressed.\n\nDoctor: Do you often notice your muscles feeling tense?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really noticed that. I mean, as a dentist, I get a little stiff in the neck sometimes just from leaning over the chair all day, but... no, nothing out of the ordinary. I don't feel like my muscles are tight or clenching up from all the worrying, no.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: Yes, I have. Most nights I actually get into bed feeling pretty tired, but as soon as the house gets quiet, my brain just starts up. I start replaying patient transfers from the office, or going over money details in my head. And even when I do manage to fall asleep, I'm usually wide awake by four or five in the morning. I just wake up feeling like I never really settled down or got any actual rest.\n\nDoctor: Have these worries been going on for at least six months?\n\nPatient: Yes, longer than that, actually. It really started when I began the formal retirement process... that was about a year and three months ago now. So, yes, it's been going on since then.\n\nDoctor: When you keep checking emails or reviewing notes, does it feel like something you have to do to relieve a nagging doubt, even when you know it probably isn’t necessary?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say that. I mean, I don't really keep checking emails or notes once I'm home. The files stay at the office. It's more that... my mind just keeps running over the details. I don't feel some urge to physically go look at the computer or double-check things over and over. I just sit there and worry about it in my head.\n\nDoctor: Before the retirement process began, were you already someone who worried like this about many different things?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I've always been a bit careful, you know, running a dental practice means you have to stay on top of things. But it was never like this. I used to be able to leave work at work and actually relax when I got home. This constant, exhausting worrying... it really only started when I started looking into retiring.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

One concern just leads right into the next one, and... eventually I just end up checking my emails, reviewing old notes, or asking the clinic the same questions all over again instead of just being able to let it go.\n\nDoctor: Do your worries mostly center on your retirement and the changes around it?\n\nPatient: Well, the retirement is a big part of it right now, but honestly, it's not just that. It feels like my brain will just latch onto whatever is in front of me. Like, if I'm driving, I start worrying about getting into an accident or if the car is making a weird noise. Or if my wife is running late coming home from the store, I'm immediately convinced something terrible happened to her. It's just... always something. The retirement is definitely loud right now, but the worrying itself just seems to cover everything.\n\nDoctor: When you’re in these periods of worry, do you often feel restless or on edge?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say that. I don't really feel restless or... jumpy, if that's what you mean. I'm not pacing the floors or anything like that. It's really just the thoughts that won't stop circling, but physically, I haven't really noticed feeling on edge.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel easily fatigued?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. By midafternoon, I just feel completely worn out. And it's a kind of tired that a nap or taking it easy in the morning doesn't really fix. It's gotten to the point where even simple things I usually enjoy, like weeding the garden or standing at my easel to paint, just feel... heavier than they used to. I just don't have the energy for them anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed lately?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say I feel depressed. I mean, I'm tired, and the worrying is exhausting, but I don't feel sad or down in the dumps. I still want to do things, like my painting or working in the yard, I just... don't have the energy for it right now because my mind is always going. But no, I don't feel depressed.\n\nDoctor: Do you often notice your muscles feeling tense?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really noticed that. I mean, as a dentist, I get a little stiff in the neck sometimes just from leaning over the chair all day, but... no, nothing out of the ordinary. I don't feel like my muscles are tight or clenching up from all the worrying, no.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: Yes, I have. Most nights I actually get into bed feeling pretty tired, but as soon as the house gets quiet, my brain just starts up. I start replaying patient transfers from the office, or going over money details in my head. And even when I do manage to fall asleep, I'm usually wide awake by four or five in the morning. I just wake up feeling like I never really settled down or got any actual rest.\n\nDoctor: Have these worries been going on for at least six months?\n\nPatient: Yes, longer than that, actually. It really started when I began the formal retirement process... that was about a year and three months ago now. So, yes, it's been going on since then.\n\nDoctor: When you keep checking emails or reviewing notes, does it feel like something you have to do to relieve a nagging doubt, even when you know it probably isn’t necessary?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say that. I mean, I don't really keep checking emails or notes once I'm home. The files stay at the office. It's more that... my mind just keeps running over the details. I don't feel some urge to physically go look at the computer or double-check things over and over. I just sit there and worry about it in my head.\n\nDoctor: Before the retirement process began, were you already someone who worried like this about many different things?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I've always been a bit careful, you know, running a dental practice means you have to stay on top of things. But it was never like this. I used to be able to leave work at work and actually relax when I got home. This constant, exhausting worrying... it really only started when I started looking into retiring.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel this worrying is mainly because of the retirement transition?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. The whole process of selling the practice, transferring the patients, dealing with the lawyers and the paperwork... it's just overwhelming. Before all that started, I was fine. But now, it's like the retirement is the only thing my brain wants to focus on, and it just spins out of control.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n15\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF43.22, F41.1, F41.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hi, it’s good to meet you. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Hi, nice to meet you too. Well, what finally got me to make an appointment is that my concentration has just gotten really unreliable lately. \n\nI’ll be at the clinic reviewing a patient's chart, or even at home just laying out a watercolor wash, and... I'll suddenly realize I’ve read the exact same line three times. My mind just completely wanders off to everything that could possibly go wrong.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying excessively about a lot of different things?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. My mind just keeps circling around all sorts of different things. Since I'm transitioning into retirement from my dental practice, I constantly worry about whether my old patients are being looked after properly, or if the practice handoff is actually financially sound. \n\nAnd then I start thinking about myself, worrying whether I’ll just become completely useless once I’m fully retired. It's just a constant loop, and honestly... the amount of worry feels out of proportion even to me.\n\nDoctor: When the worrying starts, are you able to stop or control it?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I try to reason with myself, you know, the way I would with a nervous patient in the chair. But it doesn't seem to work on me. One concern just leads right into the next one, and... eventually I just end up checking my emails, reviewing old notes, or asking the clinic the same questions all over again instead of just being able to let it go.\n\nDoctor: Do your worries mostly center on your retirement and the changes around it?\n\nPatient: Well, the retirement is a big part of it right now, but honestly, it's not just that. It feels like my brain will just latch onto whatever is in front of me. Like, if I'm driving, I start worrying about getting into an accident or if the car is making a weird noise. Or if my wife is running late coming home from the store, I'm immediately convinced something terrible happened to her. It's just... always something. The retirement is definitely loud right now, but the worrying itself just seems to cover everything.\n\nDoctor: When you’re in these periods of worry, do you often feel restless or on edge?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say that. I don't really feel restless or... jumpy, if that's what you mean. I'm not pacing the floors or anything like that. It's really just the thoughts that won't stop circling, but physically, I haven't really noticed feeling on edge.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel easily fatigued?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. By midafternoon, I just feel completely worn out. And it's a kind of tired that a nap or taking it easy in the morning doesn't really fix. It's gotten to the point where even simple things I usually enjoy, like weeding the garden or standing at my easel to paint, just feel... heavier than they used to. I just don't have the energy for them anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed lately?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say I feel depressed. I mean, I'm tired, and the worrying is exhausting, but I don't feel sad or down in the dumps. I still want to do things, like my painting or working in the yard, I just... don't have the energy for it right now because my mind is always going. But no, I don't feel depressed.\n\nDoctor: Do you often notice your muscles feeling tense?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really noticed that. I mean, as a dentist, I get a little stiff in the neck sometimes just from leaning over the chair all day, but... no, nothing out of the ordinary. I don't feel like my muscles are tight or clenching up from all the worrying, no.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: Yes, I have. Most nights I actually get into bed feeling pretty tired, but as soon as the house gets quiet, my brain just starts up. I start replaying patient transfers from the office, or going over money details in my head. And even when I do manage to fall asleep, I'm usually wide awake by four or five in the morning. I just wake up feeling like I never really settled down or got any actual rest.\n\nDoctor: Have these worries been going on for at least six months?\n\nPatient: Yes, longer than that, actually. It really started when I began the formal retirement process... that was about a year and three months ago now. So, yes, it's been going on since then.\n\nDoctor: When you keep checking emails or reviewing notes, does it feel like something you have to do to relieve a nagging doubt, even when you know it probably isn’t necessary?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say that. I mean, I don't really keep checking emails or notes once I'm home. The files stay at the office. It's more that... my mind just keeps running over the details. I don't feel some urge to physically go look at the computer or double-check things over and over. I just sit there and worry about it in my head.\n\nDoctor: Before the retirement process began, were you already someone who worried like this about many different things?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I've always been a bit careful, you know, running a dental practice means you have to stay on top of things. But it was never like this. I used to be able to leave work at work and actually relax when I got home. This constant, exhausting worrying... it really only started when I started looking into retiring.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel this worrying is mainly because of the retirement transition?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. The whole process of selling the practice, transferring the patients, dealing with the lawyers and the paperwork... it's just overwhelming. Before all that started, I was fine. But now, it's like the retirement is the only thing my brain wants to focus on, and it just spins out of control.\n\nDoctor: Has this worrying started to interfere with your day-to-day functioning?\n\nPatient: Yes, it really has. I've actually had to... well, I've had to slow down and postpone some parts of the retirement handoff. I just keep second-guessing my decisions, and I end up losing time at the clinic because I'm so stuck in my head about it all. And it's not just work, either. Even my hobbies at home... I used to enjoy painting and getting out in the garden, but lately, they just don't feel as manageable as they used to.\n\nDoctor: Outside of anything related to retirement, do you still find yourself worrying excessively?\n\nPatient: No, not really. Um, it all just seems to come back to the retirement. I haven't really noticed myself getting worked up over anything else. It's pretty much just everything tied to leaving the practice.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Hi, nice to meet you too. Well, what finally got me to make an appointment is that my concentration has just gotten really unreliable lately. \n\nI’ll be at the clinic reviewing a patient's chart, or even at home just laying out a watercolor wash, and... I'll suddenly realize I’ve read the exact same line three times. My mind just completely wanders off to everything that could possibly go wrong.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying excessively about a lot of different things?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. My mind just keeps circling around all sorts of different things. Since I'm transitioning into retirement from my dental practice, I constantly worry about whether my old patients are being looked after properly, or if the practice handoff is actually financially sound. \n\nAnd then I start thinking about myself, worrying whether I’ll just become completely useless once I’m fully retired. It's just a constant loop, and honestly... the amount of worry feels out of proportion even to me.\n\nDoctor: When the worrying starts, are you able to stop or control it?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I try to reason with myself, you know, the way I would with a nervous patient in the chair. But it doesn't seem to work on me. One concern just leads right into the next one, and... eventually I just end up checking my emails, reviewing old notes, or asking the clinic the same questions all over again instead of just being able to let it go.\n\nDoctor: Do your worries mostly center on your retirement and the changes around it?\n\nPatient: Well, the retirement is a big part of it right now, but honestly, it's not just that. It feels like my brain will just latch onto whatever is in front of me. Like, if I'm driving, I start worrying about getting into an accident or if the car is making a weird noise. Or if my wife is running late coming home from the store, I'm immediately convinced something terrible happened to her. It's just... always something. The retirement is definitely loud right now, but the worrying itself just seems to cover everything.\n\nDoctor: When you’re in these periods of worry, do you often feel restless or on edge?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say that. I don't really feel restless or... jumpy, if that's what you mean. I'm not pacing the floors or anything like that. It's really just the thoughts that won't stop circling, but physically, I haven't really noticed feeling on edge.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel easily fatigued?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. By midafternoon, I just feel completely worn out. And it's a kind of tired that a nap or taking it easy in the morning doesn't really fix. It's gotten to the point where even simple things I usually enjoy, like weeding the garden or standing at my easel to paint, just feel... heavier than they used to. I just don't have the energy for them anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed lately?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say I feel depressed. I mean, I'm tired, and the worrying is exhausting, but I don't feel sad or down in the dumps. I still want to do things, like my painting or working in the yard, I just... don't have the energy for it right now because my mind is always going. But no, I don't feel depressed.\n\nDoctor: Do you often notice your muscles feeling tense?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really noticed that. I mean, as a dentist, I get a little stiff in the neck sometimes just from leaning over the chair all day, but... no, nothing out of the ordinary. I don't feel like my muscles are tight or clenching up from all the worrying, no.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: Yes, I have. Most nights I actually get into bed feeling pretty tired, but as soon as the house gets quiet, my brain just starts up. I start replaying patient transfers from the office, or going over money details in my head. And even when I do manage to fall asleep, I'm usually wide awake by four or five in the morning. I just wake up feeling like I never really settled down or got any actual rest.\n\nDoctor: Have these worries been going on for at least six months?\n\nPatient: Yes, longer than that, actually. It really started when I began the formal retirement process... that was about a year and three months ago now. So, yes, it's been going on since then.\n\nDoctor: When you keep checking emails or reviewing notes, does it feel like something you have to do to relieve a nagging doubt, even when you know it probably isn’t necessary?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say that. I mean, I don't really keep checking emails or notes once I'm home. The files stay at the office. It's more that... my mind just keeps running over the details. I don't feel some urge to physically go look at the computer or double-check things over and over. I just sit there and worry about it in my head.\n\nDoctor: Before the retirement process began, were you already someone who worried like this about many different things?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I've always been a bit careful, you know, running a dental practice means you have to stay on top of things. But it was never like this. I used to be able to leave work at work and actually relax when I got home. This constant, exhausting worrying... it really only started when I started looking into retiring.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel this worrying is mainly because of the retirement transition?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. The whole process of selling the practice, transferring the patients, dealing with the lawyers and the paperwork... it's just overwhelming. Before all that started, I was fine. But now, it's like the retirement is the only thing my brain wants to focus on, and it just spins out of control.\n\nDoctor: Has this worrying started to interfere with your day-to-day functioning?\n\nPatient: Yes, it really has. I used to enjoy painting and getting out in the garden, but lately, they just don't feel as manageable as they used to.\n\nDoctor: Outside of anything related to retirement, do you still find yourself worrying excessively?\n\nPatient: No, not really. Um, it all just seems to come back to the retirement. I haven't really noticed myself getting worked up over anything else. It's pretty much just everything tied to leaving the practice.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying excessively about things that are unrelated to retirement?\n\nPatient: No, like I was just saying, it really is just the retirement. I don't think I'm worrying excessively about anything else right now. It all just comes back to closing up the clinic and stepping away.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Since I'm transitioning into retirement from my dental practice, I constantly worry about whether my old patients are being looked after properly, or if the practice handoff is actually financially sound. \n\nAnd then I start thinking about myself, worrying whether I’ll just become completely useless once I’m fully retired. It's just a constant loop, and honestly... the amount of worry feels out of proportion even to me.\n\nDoctor: When the worrying starts, are you able to stop or control it?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I try to reason with myself, you know, the way I would with a nervous patient in the chair. But it doesn't seem to work on me. One concern just leads right into the next one, and... eventually I just end up checking my emails, reviewing old notes, or asking the clinic the same questions all over again instead of just being able to let it go.\n\nDoctor: Do your worries mostly center on your retirement and the changes around it?\n\nPatient: Well, the retirement is a big part of it right now, but honestly, it's not just that. It feels like my brain will just latch onto whatever is in front of me. Like, if I'm driving, I start worrying about getting into an accident or if the car is making a weird noise. Or if my wife is running late coming home from the store, I'm immediately convinced something terrible happened to her. It's just... always something. The retirement is definitely loud right now, but the worrying itself just seems to cover everything.\n\nDoctor: When you’re in these periods of worry, do you often feel restless or on edge?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say that. I don't really feel restless or... jumpy, if that's what you mean. I'm not pacing the floors or anything like that. It's really just the thoughts that won't stop circling, but physically, I haven't really noticed feeling on edge.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel easily fatigued?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. By midafternoon, I just feel completely worn out. And it's a kind of tired that a nap or taking it easy in the morning doesn't really fix. It's gotten to the point where even simple things I usually enjoy, like weeding the garden or standing at my easel to paint, just feel... heavier than they used to. I just don't have the energy for them anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed lately?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say I feel depressed. I mean, I'm tired, and the worrying is exhausting, but I don't feel sad or down in the dumps. I still want to do things, like my painting or working in the yard, I just... don't have the energy for it right now because my mind is always going. But no, I don't feel depressed.\n\nDoctor: Do you often notice your muscles feeling tense?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really noticed that. I mean, as a dentist, I get a little stiff in the neck sometimes just from leaning over the chair all day, but... no, nothing out of the ordinary. I don't feel like my muscles are tight or clenching up from all the worrying, no.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: Yes, I have. Most nights I actually get into bed feeling pretty tired, but as soon as the house gets quiet, my brain just starts up. I start replaying patient transfers from the office, or going over money details in my head. And even when I do manage to fall asleep, I'm usually wide awake by four or five in the morning. I just wake up feeling like I never really settled down or got any actual rest.\n\nDoctor: Have these worries been going on for at least six months?\n\nPatient: Yes, longer than that, actually. It really started when I began the formal retirement process... that was about a year and three months ago now. So, yes, it's been going on since then.\n\nDoctor: When you keep checking emails or reviewing notes, does it feel like something you have to do to relieve a nagging doubt, even when you know it probably isn’t necessary?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say that. I mean, I don't really keep checking emails or notes once I'm home. The files stay at the office. It's more that... my mind just keeps running over the details. I don't feel some urge to physically go look at the computer or double-check things over and over. I just sit there and worry about it in my head.\n\nDoctor: Before the retirement process began, were you already someone who worried like this about many different things?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I've always been a bit careful, you know, running a dental practice means you have to stay on top of things. But it was never like this. I used to be able to leave work at work and actually relax when I got home. The whole process of selling the practice, transferring the patients, dealing with the lawyers and the paperwork... it's just overwhelming. Before all that started, I was fine. But now, it's like the retirement is the only thing my brain wants to focus on, and it just spins out of control.\n\nDoctor: Has this worrying started to interfere with your day-to-day functioning?\n\nPatient: Yes, it really has. I've actually had to... well, I've had to slow down and postpone some parts of the retirement handoff. I just keep second-guessing my decisions, and I end up losing time at the clinic because I'm so stuck in my head about it all. And it's not just work, either. Even my hobbies at home... I used to enjoy painting and getting out in the garden, but lately, they just don't feel as manageable as they used to.\n\nDoctor: Outside of anything related to retirement, do you still find yourself worrying excessively?\n\nPatient: No, not really. Um, it all just seems to come back to the retirement. I haven't really noticed myself getting worked up over anything else. It's pretty much just everything tied to leaving the practice.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying excessively about things that are unrelated to retirement?\n\nPatient: No, like I was just saying, it really is just the retirement. I don't think I'm worrying excessively about anything else right now. It all just comes back to closing up the clinic and stepping away.\n\nDoctor: If the retirement process were resolved, do you think the worrying would mostly go away?\n\nPatient: I'm not entirely sure. I mean, I'd like to think so. Since all of this really started with handing over the practice, it makes sense that it would stop once the whole thing is finally resolved. But... well, I guess I can't say for absolute certain until it actually happens.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. It really started when I began the formal retirement process... that was about a year and three months ago now. So, yes, it's been going on since then.\n\nDoctor: When you keep checking emails or reviewing notes, does it feel like something you have to do to relieve a nagging doubt, even when you know it probably isn’t necessary?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say that. I mean, I don't really keep checking emails or notes once I'm home. The files stay at the office. It's more that... my mind just keeps running over the details. I don't feel some urge to physically go look at the computer or double-check things over and over. I just sit there and worry about it in my head.\n\nDoctor: Before the retirement process began, were you already someone who worried like this about many different things?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I've always been a bit careful, you know, running a dental practice means you have to stay on top of things. But it was never like this. I used to be able to leave work at work and actually relax when I got home. This constant, exhausting worrying... it really only started when I started looking into retiring.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel this worrying is mainly because of the retirement transition?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. The whole process of selling the practice, transferring the patients, dealing with the lawyers and the paperwork... it's just overwhelming. Before all that started, I was fine. But now, it's like the retirement is the only thing my brain wants to focus on, and it just spins out of control.\n\nDoctor: Has this worrying started to interfere with your day-to-day functioning?\n\nPatient: Yes, it really has. I've actually had to... well, I've had to slow down and postpone some parts of the retirement handoff. I just keep second-guessing my decisions, and I end up losing time at the clinic because I'm so stuck in my head about it all. And it's not just work, either. Even my hobbies at home... I used to enjoy painting and getting out in the garden, but lately, they just don't feel as manageable as they used to.\n\nDoctor: Outside of anything related to retirement, do you still find yourself worrying excessively?\n\nPatient: No, not really. Um, it all just seems to come back to the retirement. I haven't really noticed myself getting worked up over anything else. It's pretty much just everything tied to leaving the practice.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying excessively about things that are unrelated to retirement?\n\nPatient: No, like I was just saying, it really is just the retirement. I don't think I'm worrying excessively about anything else right now. I mean, I've been a dentist for decades, so stepping away from the practice and figuring out all the details... that's what started all this.
